=== PROMPT VARIANT ===
r1

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given a permutation P=(P_1,P_2,\dots,P_N) of (1,2,\dots,N).
You want to satisfy P_i=i for all i=1,2,\dots,N by performing the following operation zero or more times:

- Choose an integer k such that 1 \leq k \leq N. If k \geq 2, sort the 1-st through (k-1)-th terms of P in ascending order. Then, if k \leq N-1, sort the (k+1)-th through N-th terms of P in ascending order.

It can be proved that under the constraints of this problem, it is possible to satisfy P_i=i for all i=1,2,\dots,N with a finite number of operations for any P. Find the minimum number of operations required.
You have T test cases to solve.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
T
\mathrm{case}_1
\vdots
\mathrm{case}_T

Each case is given in the following format:
N
P_1 P_2 \dots P_N

Output

Print T lines. The i-th line should contain the answer for the i-th test case.

Constraints


- 1 \leq T \leq 10^5
- 3 \leq N \leq 2 \times 10^5
- P is a permutation of (1,2,\dots,N).
- All input values are integers.
- The sum of N across the test cases in a single input is at most 2 \times 10^5.

Sample Input 1

3
5
2 1 3 5 4
3
1 2 3
7
3 2 1 7 5 6 4

Sample Output 1

1
0
2

For the first test case,

- 
Performing the operation with k=1 results in P becoming (2,1,3,4,5).

- 
Performing the operation with k=2 results in P becoming (2,1,3,4,5).

- 
Performing the operation with k=3 results in P becoming (1,2,3,4,5).

- 
Performing the operation with k=4 results in P becoming (1,2,3,5,4).

- 
Performing the operation with k=5 results in P becoming (1,2,3,5,4).


Specifically, performing the operation with k=3 results in P satisfying P_i=i for all i=1,2,\dots,5. Therefore, the minimum number of operations required is 1.
For the third test case, performing the operation with k=4 followed by k=3 results in P changing as (3,2,1,7,5,6,4) \rightarrow (1,2,3,7,4,5,6) \rightarrow (1,2,3,4,5,6,7).

```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Let's formalize operation.

Let P be current permutation. Operation at k:
- For i<k: P_i becomes the sorted list of elements originally in positions <k.
- P_k unchanged.
- For i>k: P_i becomes sorted list of elements originally in positions >k.

Equivalent: Choose pivot index k. Let L = set of elements in positions <k, R = set in positions >k, x=P_k. New array: sorted(L) followed by x followed by sorted(R).

This operation is deterministic for each k. It may be idempotent? If choose same k twice, second time left positions contain sorted(L) and right sorted(R); sorting again no change, pivot same. So yes.

Need min number of operations.

Observation: Operation at k "separates" by value? Since sorted(L) places smaller values left of pivot, sorted(R) larger values right of pivot. But L/R are arbitrary sets not necessarily intervals of values.

Let's analyze operation algebraically.

At any state, define for each position i value P_i. Operation at k:
- P_k remains same.
- Left side sorted ascending by value; right side sorted ascending.

Important: Sorting ascending by value means if we consider values, the operation places the smallest elements of the left side in the leftmost positions, etc. It is like applying a permutation within each side that is the unique order-preserving map from positions to values.

Maybe there is an invariant? Since sorting ascending is monotone, maybe after first operation, array has some "unimodal" property? Let's see.

After operation at k, left part increasing, right part increasing. The whole array may have a descent at k? Since pivot value x arbitrary; left values all from L, right from R. It is not necessarily sorted globally. But left is increasing, right increasing. Thus array consists of two increasing runs separated by pivot. Let's call "2-sorted" or "bitonic" with one pivot. Actually if left max < x < right min then globally sorted identity if sets appropriate. But generally left increasing, right increasing.

If we perform operation at k on an array that is already two increasing runs? It sorts one side maybe.

Maybe min operations corresponds to number of "runs" in some decomposition? Let's examine.

Operation at k sorts prefix and suffix, so after operation, the array is "k-sorted": P_1<...<P_{k-1} and P_{k+1}<...<P_N. P_k arbitrary. Let's call state "k-good" if both sides sorted ascending. After any operation at k, state is k-good. If choose same k again, no change. If choose different k, new state may be k'-good.

Identity is k-good for every k? Identity has both sides sorted for any k, yes.

Thus every operation produces a state with at most two increasing runs (excluding pivot). Starting arbitrary. Need reach identity.

Maybe min operations is distance in directed graph where from any state, choose k to get k-good state. Since operation deterministic, after first operation state is k-good for chosen k. From a k-good state, applying operation at l yields l-good state. Maybe we can characterize transitions between k-good states.

Let's define a "pivot-good" state S(k): P_1<...<P_{k-1}, P_{k+1}<...<P_N. Identity is S(k) for all k.

If current state is S(k), what happens when choose l?

Case l=k: no change.
Case l<k: Operation at l sorts prefix positions <l and suffix positions >l. Since current state has P_1..P_{k-1} increasing and P_{k+1}..P_N increasing. The suffix positions >l includes:
- positions l+1..k-1 (part of left increasing run)
- pivot P_k

Let's prove one-step condition carefully:
Operation with k yields identity iff:
- P_k = k (pivot unchanged)
- prefix positions <k after sorting are 1..k-1. Since prefix length k-1 and values distinct, this is equivalent to all values in prefix < k (because then they are exactly 1..k-1). Since P_k=k, also P_k not in prefix. So max_{i<k} P_i < k.
Actually if max prefix < k and P_k=k, then prefix values are k-1 distinct numbers from 1..k-1, hence exactly 1..k-1. Right values automatically k+1..N and sorted. So yes.
For k=1, max empty=0<1 and P1=1; operation sorts suffix, identity.

Let's verify the lemma for right witness in Q after op_k more rigorously, because it's crucial for P_N=1/P1!=N and for one-step condition maybe.

Lemma: Let Q = op_k(P). For j>k, Q_j=j iff max(P_1,...,P_k) < j. Actually we need only if for no witness; and sufficiency for answer2. Let's prove both directions:
- If M=max_{i≤k} P_i < j, then all values in positions 1..k are < j. Among values 1..j-1 (there are j-1), exactly k are in positions ≤k, so exactly j-1-k are in positions >k. The sorted right part has j-k-1 elements smaller than j, so its (j-k)-th element is j. Thus Q_j=j. Prefix max before j is <j because left sorted max ≤M<j, pivot ≤M<j, and right elements before j are <j. So one-step.
- Conversely, if Q_j=j, then since Q_j is in right sorted part, the number of elements in R smaller than j is j-k-1. Total values <j is j-1, so number of values in positions≤k that are <j is k. Since there are exactly k positions≤k, all of them are <j, hence M<j. Good. So equivalence.

For j<k, Q_j=j iff L contains 1..j. We don't need full.

For pivot j=k, one-step condition is P_k=k and max_{i<k}P_i<k, same as original one-step at k. Because Q_k=P_k and prefix max before k = max L = max_{i<k}P_i. If this holds, Q identity actually? But in Q one-step, if j=k and condition holds, then original one-step at k holds. Thus if answer1 false, no pivot witness. Good.

Let's think about edge cases:
- T up to 1e5, sum N 2e5. Use sys.stdin.buffer.read split ints for speed.

Implementation:
import sys
def solve():
    data=list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    it=iter(data)
    T=next(it)
    out=[]
    for _ in range(T):
        N=next(it)
        P=[next(it) for _ in range(N)]
        # identity
        is_id=True
        one=False
        max_before=0
        for idx, val in enumerate(P, start=1):
            if val != idx:
                is_id=False
            if val == idx and max_before < idx:
                one=True
            if val > max_before:
                max_before=val
        if is_id:
            out.append("0")
        elif one:
            out.append("1")
        else:
            if P[-1] == 1:
                if P[0] == N:
                    out.append("3")
                else:
                    out.append("2")
            else:
                out.append("2")
    sys.stdout.write("\n".join(out))
if __name__=="__main__": solve()

Let's maybe implement:
def solve():
    import sys
    input_data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    t = input_data[0]
    idx = 1
    ans = []
    for _ in range(t):
        n = input_data[idx]; idx += 1
        P = input_data[idx:idx+n]; idx += n
        is_id = True
        one = False
        mx = 0
        for i, v in enumerate(P, 1):
            if v != i:
                is_id = False
            if v == i and mx < i:
                one = True
            if v > mx:
                mx = v
        if is_id:
            ans.append("0")
        elif one:
            ans.append("1")
        else:
            if P[0] == n and P[-1] == 1:
                ans.append("3")
            else:
                ans.append("2")
    print("\n".join(ans))

Now, let's maybe write final code elegantly:
import sys

def main():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    t = data[0]
    idx = 1
    res = []
    for _ in range(t):
        n = data[idx]; idx += 1
        p = data[idx:idx+n]; idx += n

        is_identity = True
        one_step = False
        mx = 0
        for i, v in enumerate(p, 1):
            if v != i:
                is_identity = False
            if v == i and mx < i:
                one_step = True
            if v > mx:
                mx = v

        if is_identity:
            res.append("0")
        elif one_step:
            res.append("1")
        else:
            if p[0] == n and p[-1] == 1:
                res.append("3")
            else:
                res.append("2")
    sys.stdout.write("\n".join(res))

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

def solve():
 data=...
 t=data[0]; idx=1; out=[]
 for _ in range(t):
  n=data[idx]; idx+=1
  p=data[idx:idx+n]; idx+=n
  mx=0; one=False; ident=True
  for i,v in enumerate(p,1):
   if v!=i: ident=False
   if v==i and mx<i: one=True
   mx=max(mx,v)
  if ident: out.append('0')
  elif one: out.append('1')
  elif p[0]==n and p[-1]==1: out.append('3')
  else: out.append('2')
 print...

Now, let's maybe think if there is any issue with "one_step"
</think>

=== ANSWER ===
```python
import sys

def main():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    t = data[0]
    idx = 1
    ans = []

    for _ in range(t):
        n = data[idx]
        idx += 1
        p = data[idx:idx + n]
        idx += n

        is_identity = True
        one_step = False
        mx = 0

        for i, v in enumerate(p, 1):
            if v != i:
                is_identity = False
            if v == i and mx < i:
                one_step = True
            if v > mx:
                mx = v

        if is_identity:
            ans.append("0")
        elif one_step:
            ans.append("1")
        else:
            if p[0] == n and p[-1] == 1:
                ans.append("3")
            else:
                ans.append("2")

    sys.stdout.write("\n".join(ans))

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```
